mistral-large-4/05-technical-writing/WEBHOOKS.md

Orbit Webhooks

Webhooks let your service receive event notifications from Orbit as HTTP POST requests. This guide covers how to verify deliveries, how delivery and retries behave, how to handle duplicates and ordering, key rotation, and common problems.

Throughout this guide, Guaranteed marks behavior the platform commits to, and Recommended marks our advice, which is not enforced.

1. Verifying signatures

Every delivery includes these headers:

  • X-Orbit-Event — the unique event ID.
  • X-Orbit-Type — the event type.
  • X-Orbit-Timestamp — Unix timestamp (seconds) at signing time.
  • X-Orbit-Signature — v1=<hex>, an HMAC-SHA256 signature.

Guaranteed: the signature is HMAC-SHA256, keyed with your webhook secret, computed over <timestamp>.<raw request body> — the timestamp header value, a literal dot, then the exact raw body bytes as received. Verify against the raw bytes; do not parse and re-serialize the JSON first.

Older documentation said the signature was computed over the parsed JSON object. That note is stale and conflicts with the current implementation. Always sign and verify the raw request body exactly as received.

Guaranteed: your webhook secret is shown exactly once, when the endpoint is created or the secret is rotated. Store it immediately.

Minimal verification example (Python, no framework):

import hashlib
import hmac
import os

def verify(raw_body: bytes, timestamp: str, signature: str) -> bool:
    secret = os.environ["ORBIT_WEBHOOK_SECRET"].encode()
    expected = hmac.new(
        secret, timestamp.encode() + b"." + raw_body, hashlib.sha256
    ).hexdigest()
    received = signature.removeprefix("v1=")
    return hmac.compare_digest(expected, received)

Reject the request if verification fails, and use a constant-time comparison (hmac.compare_digest above); a plain == leaks timing information.

Recommended: also reject deliveries whose X-Orbit-Timestamp is too old. The platform does not publish a maximum accepted age, so choose a tolerance (for example, five minutes) with your security team. This is a recommendation, not a platform guarantee.

2. Delivery semantics

Guaranteed:

  • Deliveries are HTTP POST requests with a JSON body.
  • Your endpoint has 10 seconds to return any 2xx status. The response body is ignored.
  • Payloads are at most 512 KiB.
  • The User-Agent header is Orbit-Hooks/2.

Recommended: return 2xx as soon as you have durably stored the raw body, then process asynchronously. Verifying and fully processing within 10 seconds is possible but leaves little margin for slow dependencies.

3. Retries

Guaranteed:

  • We retry a delivery on timeout, on network error, and on HTTP 408, 425, 429, or any 5xx.
  • We do not retry other 4xx responses; those are treated as permanent failures.
  • After the initial attempt there are five retries, at roughly 1 minute, 5 minutes, 30 minutes, 2 hours, and 12 hours.

Recommended: treat the status you return as a decision. A 4xx (other than 408/425/429) means "do not send this event again"; a 5xx or a timeout means "try again later." Log the X-Orbit-Event ID with every failure so you can correlate with the retry schedule.

4. Ordering and idempotency

Guaranteed:

  • Duplicate deliveries are possible.
  • Delivery order usually follows event creation order, but it is not guaranteed, because concurrent retries can arrive out of order.
  • The replay feature can resend an event for up to 7 days, and a replayed event keeps the same X-Orbit-Event ID.

Recommended: make your handler idempotent by using X-Orbit-Event as the idempotency key — store processed event IDs and skip repeats. Do not infer creation order from delivery order; if your logic depends on sequence, use sequence data in the payload.

5. Key rotation

Guaranteed:

  • During rotation, two secrets are valid simultaneously for 24 hours.
  • The dashboard labels them current and previous.

Recommended: deploy the new secret first and verify with it, accept signatures from either secret during the 24-hour overlap, and remove the old secret only after in-flight deliveries have drained. Because each secret is shown only once, store it at creation time.

6. Operational advice

  • Never log your webhook secret, and avoid logging full payloads that contain sensitive data. Log event IDs and types instead.
  • Keep the endpoint reachable: five retries over roughly 14.5 hours are the entire safety net for a failing endpoint.
  • Recommended: alert on repeated signature-verification failures; they usually indicate a wrong or stale secret (see the table below).

7. Troubleshooting

Symptom Likely cause What to do
Signature verification fails on every event Wrong secret, or verifying re-serialized JSON instead of the raw body Verify timestamp + "." + raw body; confirm you use the current secret
Signature fails only after rotation Old secret removed too early, or new secret not yet deployed Both secrets are valid for 24 h; accept either during the overlap
No deliveries at all Endpoint unreachable, TLS/firewall issue, or no 2xx within 10 s Check connectivity; confirm a 2xx response within 10 s
Events stop after ~14.5 hours of failures All five retries exhausted Fix the endpoint, then replay the event from the dashboard (7-day window)
Duplicate processing Retries or a manual replay (same event ID) Deduplicate on X-Orbit-Event
Events arrive out of order Concurrent retries; order is not guaranteed Do not rely on delivery order; use payload sequence data
Endpoint returns 4xx and events stop 4xx other than 408/425/429 is not retried Fix the bug, then replay the event from the dashboard

8. Open questions

  • Redirects: it is not yet confirmed whether the delivery client follows HTTP redirects. Our security team believes it does not, but the implementation owner has not confirmed. Until product confirms, do not rely on redirects — expose the final endpoint URL directly.

  • Inline caution that a short freshness cutoff could reject retries/replays
  • Durable insert-with-unique-constraint dedupe advice

gpt-6.2-sol/05-technical-writing/RESPONSE.md

Editorial choices and product questions

Editorial choices

  • Used the source note identified as current implementation for the signing input. The older parsed-JSON instruction conflicts with that note, and the shorter HMAC-over-raw-bytes description omits the timestamp prefix. The guide specifies the timestamp header value, a literal dot, and the original body bytes.
  • Put Orbit's documented behavior alongside separate receiver actions. The retry schedule is approximate, and the observed creation order is described alongside the absence of an ordering guarantee. The guide gives durable acceptance, idempotency, safe logging, and direct endpoint configuration as integration recommendations.
  • Made the Python verifier independent of a web framework. It accepts the documented single-signature shape, handles malformed headers, and compares the supplied digest with each configured key using constant-time comparison. It takes secret bytes so the example makes no assumption about how the displayed secret is encoded.
  • Treated UI replay as another delivery of the same event because it preserves the event ID. The guide recommends using the same deduplication path for replay and automatic retries.

Product confirmation needed

  • Redirects and other statuses. Does Orbit follow any 3xx response? How does it classify responses outside the specified success, retryable, and terminal categories?
  • Retry timing. Are the five approximate delays measured from the initial attempt or from each preceding attempt? Does a Retry-After header affect the schedule?
  • Timestamps and replay. Is the timestamp regenerated for each automatic retry and UI replay? When does the seven-day UI replay window start? Does a UI replay get its own retry schedule?
  • Rotation and secret format. During the overlap, which secret signs new deliveries, pending retries, and UI replays? Confirm whether Orbit always emits exactly one v1 signature and whether the displayed secret should be used literally or decoded into key bytes.

gpt-6.2-sol/05-technical-writing/WEBHOOKS.md

Orbit webhook delivery guide

Orbit sends webhook events as HTTP POST requests with JSON bodies up to 512 KiB. Use an HTTPS endpoint and keep the raw request body available so your handler can verify the signature before it parses the JSON.

Request format and verification

Header Value
X-Orbit-Event Event ID. Use this value to recognize repeat deliveries.
X-Orbit-Type Event type.
X-Orbit-Timestamp Unix timestamp in seconds.
X-Orbit-Signature v1=<hex>, a hex-encoded HMAC-SHA-256 digest.
User-Agent Orbit-Hooks/2.

Orbit computes the HMAC using the webhook secret as the key. The signed message is the timestamp header value as received, followed by a literal dot (.) and the raw request body bytes. Capture those bytes before JSON parsing so that whitespace, encoding, and line endings stay intact. The event and type headers sit outside the signed message.

Older Orbit documentation described signing parsed JSON. Update any verifier built from that documentation to use the current signing format.

Minimal Python example

Pass the raw body as bytes, the timestamp and signature header values as strings, and a collection of webhook secrets as byte strings. The example accepts a single v1 signature and uses a constant-time digest comparison for each configured secret.

import hmac
import re


def verify_webhook(raw_body, timestamp_header, signature_header, secrets):
    timestamp = timestamp_header or ""
    signature = re.fullmatch(r"v1=([0-9a-fA-F]{64})", signature_header or "")
    if not re.fullmatch(r"[0-9]+", timestamp) or signature is None:
        return False

    signed_message = timestamp.encode("ascii") + b"." + raw_body
    received = bytes.fromhex(signature.group(1))
    verified = False
    for secret in secrets:
        expected = hmac.digest(secret, signed_message, "sha256")
        verified |= hmac.compare_digest(expected, received)
    return verified

If verification fails, reject the request with a status such as 400 before processing it. If you enforce timestamp freshness, confirm how Orbit sets timestamps on retries and UI replays before choosing a cutoff. If Orbit reuses the original timestamp, a short cutoff could reject legitimate later attempts.

Delivery and retry behavior

Your endpoint has 10 seconds to return any HTTP 2xx response. Orbit treats that response as a successful delivery and ignores the response body.

Orbit retries on timeouts, network errors, HTTP 408, 425, 429, and 5xx responses. Other 4xx responses end automatic retries for that delivery. Orbit can make up to five retries after the initial attempt. The approximate retry schedule lists 1 minute, 5 minutes, 30 minutes, 2 hours, and 12 hours. The reference point for those times still needs product confirmation.

Redirect handling also requires product confirmation. Register the final endpoint URL directly and check that your proxy and application serve that path without a redirect.

Ordering and idempotency

Deliveries usually follow creation order. Orbit provides no ordering guarantee, and concurrent retries can deliver an older event after a newer one. Duplicate deliveries are possible, including when your handler records an event but Orbit times out before receiving the acknowledgment. The replay UI can resend an event for seven days and preserves the event ID.

Use a durable record keyed by X-Orbit-Event to deduplicate concurrent attempts, retries, and UI replays. For example, insert the event and its pending work into a database in one transaction, using a unique constraint on the event ID. Acknowledge with 2xx once the work is durable. Return 2xx for a verified duplicate once you know the event is already recorded. Keep deduplication records through the retry and replay windows.

Process the pending work asynchronously and make its side effects idempotent. For example, pass the event ID as an idempotency key to downstream APIs that support it. If processing depends on the latest state, use a version check or reconcile with the source of truth before applying an update.

Key rotation

Orbit shows each webhook secret once. Store it in your secret manager when it is displayed. After rotation, the current and previous secrets are both valid during a 24-hour overlap. The UI labels them Current and Previous.

Prepare your verifier to accept either configured secret during the overlap. Add the new secret to the receiver as soon as it is available and check that verified deliveries continue. Remove the previous secret from the receiver once the overlap ends.

Operating your endpoint

Configure your proxy and application to accept webhook bodies up to 512 KiB. Keep the acknowledgment path fast by verifying and recording the event before handing off longer work. Return a retryable status such as 503 if a temporary failure prevents durable acceptance. Monitor response codes, acknowledgment latency, verification failures, and processing backlog. Log event IDs and enough context to diagnose failures while keeping secrets and full sensitive payloads out of logs.

Troubleshooting

Symptom What to check
Signatures fail after a handler or proxy change Ensure the verifier receives the original body bytes and uses the timestamp header, dot, and body in that order.
Verification failures start after rotation Check that the receiver has the new secret and still accepts the previous secret during the overlap.
Orbit retries an event you already processed Check whether a 2xx reached Orbit within 10 seconds and whether the event ID was recorded before acknowledgment.
A delivery ends after one attempt Check whether the endpoint returned a 4xx other than 408, 425, or 429.
The endpoint returns a redirect Check the registered URL and route directly to the handler.
Replaying an event does not repeat a side effect Check the deduplication record. UI replays preserve the event ID.

Strengths

  • Clear Guarantee/Recommendation per section
  • Open questions include retry-id reuse and which secret signs during rotation
  • Advises durable dedupe storage and conditional writes

Weaknesses

  • Example enforces 300 s tolerance and takes one secret
Evidence the grader checked

  • h2.py: G fresh=True, 1h-old retry=False

deepseek-v4.1-flash/05-technical-writing/RESPONSE.md

RESPONSE — editorial notes for WEBHOOKS.md

Approach

The source notes are an engineer's shorthand: 20 lines covering protocol, retry policy, ordering, rotation and limits, with one flagged internal contradiction. The guide is written for a customer's backend developer who has already registered an endpoint and now has to make it correct under failure, so it is organised by the order in which they meet each problem: what arrives, verify, respond, retries, ordering, duplicates, rotation, replay, operations, troubleshooting. Reference-style material (headers, limits) is front-loaded; narrative is kept out of the tables.

Length is 1,097 words by wc -w, against a 700–1,100 target. Excluding the code block and tables the prose is closer to 800, so the guide is not padded by markup.

Guarantees versus recommendations

Every behavioural claim is labelled Guarantee or Recommendation at the point of use, and the convention is defined in the first screen so a reader who skips the intro still reads the labels correctly. This was the main structural decision. Mixing the two is the failure mode that damages trust in webhook documentation: a customer who builds deduplication on a suggestion that later changes, or who assumes a documented retry schedule is exact, files a support ticket. Labelling inline rather than in a separate "best practices" section also forces each recommendation to sit next to the guarantee it qualifies.

Three items are deliberately downgraded to recommendations even though the notes state them in declarative form:

  • Timestamp freshness. The notes give the header but no tolerance window. Publishing "reject after 5 minutes" as a rule would invent a contract term, so the 300-second default is presented as the integrator's choice, with a note that Orbit publishes no window.
  • Approximate retry intervals. "Roughly" is preserved verbatim in spirit: the schedule is given as approximate, with an explicit instruction not to depend on exact arrival times.
  • Dedupe retention of seven days. Seven days is derived from the replay window, not stated for dedupe itself, so it is framed as a recommendation with the derivation visible.

Resolving the contradiction

The notes contain one contradiction: old documentation says the signature covers parsed JSON, while the current implementation signs timestamp + "." + raw body. The guide follows the current implementation and says so explicitly, including that the old note is stale. Two reasons: the implementation is authoritative over documentation, and signing parsed JSON is not implementable safely in general because re-serialisation does not reproduce original bytes. Rather than silently dropping the old behaviour, the guide names it and explains why it is wrong, so a customer who finds the old page in search results is not left with two apparently valid answers. The example hashes raw bytes, and the troubleshooting table's first row is the failure this contradiction produces in the field.

The example was executed against seven cases — valid signature, lowercase header names, tampered signature, tampered body, stale timestamp, missing headers, malformed timestamp — and returns the expected result for each.

Deliberate omissions

  • No event-type catalogue or payload schema. The notes name no event types, and inventing plausible ones (invoice.paid) would create a contract the service does not have.
  • No Retry-After behaviour. 429 is stated as retryable, but nothing says whether Retry-After is honoured. Mentioning it would imply support.
  • No claim of eventual delivery. The notes describe retry mechanics, not a delivery guarantee, so the guide describes attempts and says the post-retry outcome is undocumented.
  • No HTTPS, IP allowlist or endpoint-registration detail. Nothing in the notes covers provisioning; the guide opens where the notes open.

Open questions for product

These are listed in the guide under "Needs product confirmation" with the conservative reading, and repeated here with why each matters:

  1. Are 3xx responses followed? Security believes no; the implementation owner has not confirmed. This changes whether a receiver behind a redirect silently loses events or fails loudly. Until confirmed, the guide tells readers to return a final status.
  2. What happens after the sixth attempt fails? No retention, dead-letter, alerting or endpoint-disabling behaviour is documented. Customers need this to decide whether they must reconcile independently.
  3. Which secret signs during rotation, and can the header carry two v1= values? The notes say two secrets are valid for 24 hours and that the UI labels them, but not what is sent. If only one signature is sent and it is the previous secret, a receiver that checks only "current" breaks mid-window.
  4. Do retries reuse the event id? Replay is documented to reuse it; retries are only implied by "duplicates possible". If retries used new ids, dedupe on the event id would be unsound — this is the highest-impact unknown in the document.
  5. Is the 512 KiB limit enforced by truncation or by rejection? The notes give the maximum but not the behaviour at the boundary. The guide avoids stating either.

Items 2, 3 and 4 need an answer from the implementation owner before the guide can lose its "Needs product confirmation" section; item 1 needs a security decision; item 5 is a documentation gap that probably resolves to "rejected".

deepseek-v4.1-flash/05-technical-writing/WEBHOOKS.md

Orbit webhooks: delivery and verification guide

This guide covers what you build: verifying requests, responding correctly, handling retries and duplicates, and rotating secrets.

Guarantee marks behaviour Orbit commits to today; Recommendation marks advice that is not part of the contract.

What Orbit sends

Each event is a POST with a UTF-8 JSON body of at most 512 KiB and these headers:

Header Value
X-Orbit-Event Opaque event id, stable for the life of the event
X-Orbit-Type Event type
X-Orbit-Timestamp Unix time in seconds, decimal
X-Orbit-Signature v1=<hex HMAC-SHA-256>
User-Agent Orbit-Hooks/2

Header names are case-insensitive.

Verifying the signature

Guarantee. The signature is HMAC-SHA-256, keyed with your signing secret, over the timestamp, a literal . (0x2E), and the raw request body bytes. The header value is the hex digest prefixed with v1=.

Older documentation says the signature covers parsed JSON. That is stale: the current implementation signs the timestamp and raw body. Signing parsed JSON would be unsafe anyway, because re-serialising does not reproduce the original bytes. Verify before you parse.

Minimal Python example, standard library only:

import hashlib, hmac, time

def verify(secret: bytes, headers, raw_body: bytes, tolerance: int = 300) -> bool:
    h = {k.lower(): v for k, v in headers.items()}   # lookup is case-insensitive
    ts = h.get("x-orbit-timestamp")
    signature = h.get("x-orbit-signature", "")
    try:
        fresh = abs(time.time() - int(ts)) <= tolerance
    except (TypeError, ValueError):
        return False                       # missing or malformed timestamp
    if not fresh or not signature:
        return False
    signed = ts.encode("ascii") + b"." + raw_body
    expected = "v1=" + hmac.new(secret, signed, hashlib.sha256).hexdigest()
    return hmac.compare_digest(expected.lower(), signature.lower())  # constant-time

Guarantee. Signature comparison is constant-time on our side; use an equivalent primitive, never == on the digest.

The timestamp check is a Recommendation. Rejecting requests older than a few minutes limits replay; Orbit publishes no tolerance window, so 300 seconds is a reasonable default. Keep clocks synchronised.

Most JSON parsers discard the original bytes. Capture them explicitly — express.raw({ type: "application/json" }), request.get_data() — and parse after verification.

Responding

Guarantee. You have 10 seconds from arrival to return a complete response. Any 2xx counts as success, including 202 and 204. The response body is ignored.

Recommendation. Verify, persist, enqueue, then return 2xx; keep slow work out of the handler. A 2xx is final — you cannot report a later failure — so return it only once the event is durably accepted.

Retries

Guarantee. An attempt is retried when it times out, fails at the network level (DNS, TCP, TLS), or returns 408, 425, 429 or any 5xx. Other 4xx responses — 400, 401, 403, 404, 409, 410, 422 and so on — are permanent and are not retried. There are five retries after the initial attempt, at approximately 1 minute, 5 minutes, 30 minutes, 2 hours and 12 hours: six attempts over roughly 15 hours.

Recommendation. Treat the schedule as approximate; do not depend on arrival at a particular minute. If you return 429 deliberately, ensure capacity recovers inside the window.

Ordering

Guarantee. Delivery order is not guaranteed. Events usually arrive in creation order, but a concurrent retry can put a later event ahead of an earlier one still in flight.

Recommendation. Assume any two events may arrive in either order. Where order matters, use a sequence number or timestamp from the payload, or re-read state from the API instead of replaying it locally.

Duplicates and idempotency

Guarantee. Delivery is at-least-once: the same event can arrive more than once.

Recommendation. Deduplicate on X-Orbit-Event. Record ids in durable storage, not process memory, for at least seven days to cover replay. Make side effects idempotent where you can — upserts keyed on the event's subject, conditional writes — so a duplicate is harmless.

Rotating secrets

Guarantee. After a rotation, two secrets are accepted for 24 hours. The dashboard labels them current and previous; the previous secret stops being accepted when the window ends.

Recommendation. Verify against both secrets during the window — accept a match on either — and deploy that logic before you rotate, not after.

Replaying events

Guarantee. The dashboard can resend an event for up to 7 days after creation. A replay reuses the original event id and type and follows normal delivery rules.

Recommendation. Treat a replay as any other duplicate and let idempotency absorb it.

Operational advice

  • Reject unverified requests with 401 before parsing.
  • Never log the signing secret, X-Orbit-Signature, or full payloads carrying personal or payment data. Log event id, type, status and latency instead.
  • Alert on a sustained rise in 4xx responses; a wall of 401s usually means a secret or timestamp problem, not an attack.

Troubleshooting

Symptom Likely cause What to do
Every signature fails Hashing parsed JSON, or bytes a parser rewrote Capture raw bytes; sign timestamp + "." + raw_body
Fails only in production A proxy or middleware re-encoding the body Disable body rewriting on the route
Fails for a minute every hour Clock drift on the host Enable NTP; widen tolerance only as a stopgap
Requests time out More than 10 s of work in the handler Return 2xx first, process asynchronously
Same event processed twice Dedupe absent, or lost from memory on restart Persist event ids for at least 7 days
Verification breaks after rotation Only the current secret is checked Verify both for the 24-hour window
Delivery stops entirely Non-retryable 4xx, or retries exhausted Check the endpoint's response codes and history

Needs product confirmation

These points are unresolved; each gives the conservative reading.

  • Redirects. Whether 3xx responses are followed is unconfirmed. Assume not; return 2xx or a definite failure rather than redirecting.
  • After the final retry. Nothing documents what happens when all six attempts fail — whether events are retained, surfaced, or the endpoint disabled. Assume the event is not redelivered automatically.
  • Rotation detail. It is unstated which secret signs outgoing deliveries during the window, and whether the header can carry two v1= values. Verify both.
  • Retry identity. Replays are documented to reuse the event id; retries doing the same is implied by "duplicates possible" but never stated.
  • Payload limit. The 512 KiB maximum is stated, but not whether larger payloads are rejected or truncated at the boundary.

Evaluation 7.5 / 10 graded blind as submission S

Clear, labelled guide with a correct example, but it invents guarantees (ASCII bytes, lowercase hex, a delivery log), says undelivered after '~12 hours', and recommends few-minute timestamp rejection without considering retries/replays.

Strengths

  • Guarantee/Recommendation on each paragraph
  • Advice to ack unknown event types
  • Open questions include HTTPS and retry precision

Weaknesses

  • 'The digest is lowercase hex' and 'ASCII bytes' presented as guarantees
  • 'check Orbit's delivery log' invents a feature
  • Few-minute timestamp rejection recommended without retry/replay caveat; open list omits timestamp/rotation questions
Evidence the grader checked
  • WEBHOOKS.md 'Signature verification' and troubleshooting
  • harness: valid=True tamper=False badsig=False

RESPONSE.md — editorial choices and unresolved questions

Editorial choices

  1. Signature contradiction resolved toward the current implementation. SOURCE_NOTES.md contains three related claims: HMAC SHA-256 "over raw bytes"; legacy docs signing parsed JSON; and the current implementation signing timestamp + "." + raw body. I documented HMAC-SHA256(secret, "<timestamp>.<raw body>") as the guarantee, flagged the legacy parsed-JSON description as stale/conflicting, and did not invent a compatibility path. Product confirmation is requested in the guide.

  2. Guarantees vs recommendations labeled explicitly. Every normative statement carries a **Guarantee** or **Recommendation** marker so customers can tell product behavior from operational advice.

  3. Retry semantics taken literally. Retry on timeout, network error, 408, 425, 429, 5xx; all other 4xx terminal; five retries after the initial attempt at roughly 1m/5m/30m/2h/12h. The schedule is marked approximate because the notes say "roughly."

  4. Success criterion stated tightly. Any 2xx within 10 seconds; response body ignored. The 10-second timeout is presented as what triggers a retry on timeout (interpretation; see questions).

  5. Ordering kept weak. "Usually follows creation" is preserved as a soft observation, with an explicit statement that order is not guaranteed and duplicates are possible. Idempotency on X-Orbit-Event is a recommendation, not a claim that the platform deduplicates.

  6. Key rotation and replay documented with concrete windows. Two secrets for 24 hours with UI labels current/previous; replay UI for 7 days reusing the same event id. Verification against both secrets during overlap is a recommendation.

  7. Redirects left as unknown. Security's "no" is reported as a belief, not a guarantee, because the implementation owner has not confirmed. I invented no behavior and advised against relying on redirects for migration.

  8. Operational safety wording from the notes is included as required: examples warn against logging secrets or full sensitive payloads. HTTPS is framed as a recommendation only — the notes never state it is required.

  9. Nothing invented beyond the notes. No IP allowlists, content-type rules, signature-scheme version negotiation, or contractual clock-skew windows. Clock-skew rejection appears only as a recommendation whose threshold is left to the customer.

  10. Target length and shape. The guide is structured for skimming (request format, verification, retries, ordering, rotation, replay, operations, troubleshooting table, open questions) and kept within the 700–1,100 word band.

Unresolved questions (need product confirmation)

  1. Signature input. Is the signed message timestamp + "." + raw body (current implementation) or parsed JSON (legacy docs)? Please confirm and retire the stale documentation.
  2. Redirects. Are HTTP redirects followed on delivery? Security believes not; the implementation owner has not confirmed.
  3. HTTPS requirement. Must customer endpoints be HTTPS, or is HTTP acceptable? Documented as a recommendation pending confirmation.
  4. Retry schedule precision. Are 1m/5m/30m/2h/12h contractual or best-effort? Stated as approximate.
  5. 10-second timeout semantics. Is a 2xx arriving after 10 seconds treated as failure (and retried)? Assumed yes via "retry on timeout."
  6. Secret scope and labeling. Is the secret shown once per endpoint or per workspace, and how does the current/previous labeling behave with multiple endpoints?

mimo/05-technical-writing/WEBHOOKS.md

Orbit Webhooks

Orbit delivers events to your endpoint as HTTP POST requests with JSON bodies. Verify the signature before you trust a payload. This guide covers request format, verification, delivery semantics, and production operations.

Throughout, Guarantee marks documented product behavior; Recommendation marks operational advice you may choose to ignore.

Request format

Each delivery is an HTTP POST with a JSON body of at most 512 KiB and User-Agent: Orbit-Hooks/2.

Header Value
X-Orbit-Event Event id — stable across retries and replays
X-Orbit-Type Event type
X-Orbit-Timestamp Unix time in seconds (signature input)
X-Orbit-Signature v1=<hex> HMAC SHA-256 signature

Recommendation. Acknowledge event types you do not recognize with 2xx and no-op, so new types roll out without breaking you.

Signature verification

Guarantee (current implementation). X-Orbit-Signature is HMAC-SHA256 keyed by your signing secret over the ASCII bytes of "<X-Orbit-Timestamp>.<raw request body>". Note the literal . separator and that the body is the exact bytes received — not re-serialized JSON. The digest is lowercase hex, prefixed with v1=.

Conflict — requires product confirmation. Older documentation states that the signature covers parsed JSON. That description conflicts with the current implementation and is likely stale. This guide follows the current implementation (timestamp + "." + raw body). Do not implement a JSON-re-serialization scheme; see Open questions.

Guarantee. The signing secret is shown once at creation. Store it in a secret manager immediately; it cannot be re-displayed.

Minimal verification example (Python 3):

import hashlib
import hmac

def verify(secret: str, timestamp: str, body: bytes, signature_header: str) -> bool:
    message = f"{timestamp}.".encode("ascii") + body
    digest = hmac.new(secret.encode("ascii"), message, hashlib.sha256).hexdigest()
    return hmac.compare_digest(f"v1={digest}", signature_header)

Recommendation. Always compare in constant time (as above). Reject requests whose X-Orbit-Timestamp is more than a few minutes away from your clock to bound replay risk — the acceptable skew is your policy, not a product setting. Never log signing secrets or full sensitive payloads; log event ids and types only.

Delivery and retries

Guarantee. A delivery succeeds only if your endpoint returns any 2xx within 10 seconds. Response bodies are ignored.

Guarantee. Orbit retries on timeout, network errors, and HTTP 408, 425, 429, and 5xx. Any other 4xx is terminal and is not retried.

Guarantee. After the initial attempt there are five retries, scheduled roughly at 1 minute, 5 minutes, 30 minutes, 2 hours, and 12 hours. Timing is approximate.

Recommendation. Return 2xx as soon as the event is durably queued and do heavy work asynchronously, so you stay inside the 10-second window. Use 5xx or 429 for transient failures you want retried. Return a non-retryable 4xx (for example 400) only when you want Orbit to stop.

Ordering and idempotency

Guarantee. Duplicates are possible. A retry can race an earlier slow success, and the replay UI (below) intentionally resends events.

Guarantee. Delivery order is not guaranteed. Deliveries usually follow event creation order, but concurrent retries can reorder them.

Recommendation. Make handlers idempotent keyed on X-Orbit-Event: persist processed ids and no-op on repeats. Do not assume event N is processed before event N+1.

Key rotation

Guarantee. During rotation, two secrets are valid for 24 hours. The UI labels them current and previous.

Recommendation. While rotating, accept signatures from either secret; drop the previous secret when the 24-hour window closes.

Replaying events

Guarantee. For 7 days after an event, the replay UI can resend it. Replays reuse the original X-Orbit-Event id.

Recommendation. Rely on idempotent handlers so replays are free.

Operational advice

Recommendation. Expose a publicly reachable HTTPS endpoint. Secrets and payloads are sensitive.

Recommendation. Keep verification and acknowledgment fast; defer business logic past the 2xx.

Recommendation. On an invalid signature, do not process the request. Return a non-retryable 4xx and log only metadata (event id, type, timestamp) — never the secret or the full body.

Recommendation. Size handlers for 512 KiB request bodies.

Unconfirmed. Whether Orbit follows HTTP redirects on delivery is unknown. Security believes redirects are not followed; the implementation owner has not confirmed. Do not use redirects to migrate an endpoint — update the configured URL instead.

No deliveries arriving Endpoint unreachable or wrong configured URL Confirm public reachability; check Orbit's delivery log

Intermittent signature failures Clock skew, or wrong secret during rotation Check X-Orbit-Timestamp; try both secrets inside the 24-hour overlap

  2. Redirects. Unconfirmed (security: not followed; implementation owner: not confirmed). Documented as unknown; do not depend on redirects.

muse/05-technical-writing/RESPONSE.md

Response: editorial choices and open questions

Editorial choices

  • Guarantee vs. recommendation labels: Every behavior paragraph is prefixed with bold Guarantee (service commits to it per the notes) or Recommendation (receiver-side advice the service does not enforce). This keeps the required distinction visible without a separate legend the reader could miss.
  • Signature contradiction resolved toward the current implementation: The notes say old docs describe signing parsed JSON but the current implementation signs timestamp + "." + raw body. I documented only the current form as the guarantee, marked the old note stale in Section 1, and warned to verify raw bytes before parsing (re-serialization breaks signatures).
  • Conservative retry wording: The schedule is stated as nominal and approximate ("approximately ... not a timing guarantee") because the notes say "roughly". The retryable set (timeout, network error, 408, 425, 429, 5xx) and the non-retry of other 4xx are stated as guarantees, with receiver guidance on which status to return.
  • Ordering stated as no guarantee: "Usually in creation order" is explicitly downgraded to background context; the guarantee is that order is not guaranteed, with idempotency keyed on X-Orbit-Event as the recommendation.
  • No invented timestamp tolerance: The notes give no clock-skew window, so the 5-minute freshness check is framed as an example receiver policy, not a service requirement.
  • Rotation as try-current-then-previous: The notes only say two secrets are valid for 24 hours with UI labels. The fallback-then-remove pattern is presented as a recommendation, not a service behavior.
  • Replay and duplicates unified: The 7-day replay with the same event ID is placed under ordering/idempotency so readers treat replays as expected duplicates rather than new events.
  • Logging warning kept in the code section: Placed next to the verification example where a developer is most likely to add debug logging, rather than buried in operations.
  • Redirects not documented as behavior: Left as an explicit unresolved item in its own section and in troubleshooting, with the conservative advice to use a final URL.

Unresolved questions needing product confirmation

  1. Redirects: Are any 3xx responses followed (which codes, how many hops, method/body preserved)? Security believes no, but the implementation owner has not confirmed. Blocks firm guidance.
  2. Timestamp validation: Is there a sender-side or required receiver-side clock-skew window? Should receivers reject old timestamps, and at what age?
  3. Retry details: What counts as "timeout" vs. "network error" (connect vs. read, DNS failure, TLS failure)? Is there jitter on the nominal 1m/5m/30m/2h/12h schedule, and does the schedule restart after a replay?
  4. Signature versioning: Is v1 the only version receivers will see? How should unknown versions be handled, and will new versions overlap like secrets during migration?
  5. Oversize payloads: What happens above 512 KiB — never sent, truncated, or delivered with an error signal? Should receivers pre-reject by Content-Length?
  6. Replay signing secret: Is a replayed event re-signed with the current secret or the secret active at original delivery? This affects how long old secrets must be retained.

Task 05 — Technical writing: webhook delivery guide

The guide must include a minimal verification example, delivery and retry semantics, ordering/idempotency guidance, key rotation, operational advice, and a troubleshooting table. Write RESPONSE.md with your editorial choices and unresolved questions. Work only in this directory.

muse/05-technical-writing/WEBHOOKS.md

Orbit Webhooks

Use webhooks to receive event notifications over HTTPS: verification, retries, and running a reliable receiver.

Throughout this guide, Guarantee means behavior the service commits to, and Recommendation means guidance for your receiver that the service does not enforce.

1. Receiving and verifying events

Guarantee: Each delivery is an HTTPS POST with a JSON body of at most 512 KiB and these headers:

  • X-Orbit-Event: unique event ID. Replays reuse the same ID.
  • X-Orbit-Type: event type string.
  • X-Orbit-Timestamp: send time as Unix seconds.
  • X-Orbit-Signature: v1=<hex>, an HMAC SHA-256 signature.
  • User-Agent: Orbit-Hooks/2.

Guarantee: The signature is computed over the raw request bytes as:

signed_payload = <timestamp ASCII> + "." + <raw body bytes>
signature = "v1=" + hex(HMAC-SHA256(secret, signed_payload))

Verify against the raw bytes before JSON parsing. Older documentation described signing parsed JSON; that note is stale and must not be used. The current behavior signs timestamp + "." + raw body.

Guarantee: Your endpoint must return any 2xx within 10 seconds for the delivery to count as successful. The response body is ignored.

Minimal verification example (Python):

import hashlib
import hmac

def verify_orbit(secret: bytes, timestamp: str, raw_body: bytes, header: str) -> bool:
    signed = timestamp.encode("ascii") + b"." + raw_body
    expected = "v1=" + hmac.new(secret, signed, hashlib.sha256).hexdigest()
    return hmac.compare_digest(expected, header)

# In your handler: read raw_body first, then verify, then parse JSON.
# if not verify_orbit(secret, timestamp, raw_body, signature_header):
#     return 400  # do not retry; see Section 2

Recommendation: Use a constant-time comparison such as hmac.compare_digest, reject requests with a missing timestamp or signature, and reject timestamps far from your clock (for example, older than 5 minutes). No clock-skew window is published, so treat your tolerance as your own policy.

Recommendation: Never log the webhook secret, the full signature header alongside the body, or full sensitive payloads. Log the event ID, type, timestamp, and verification outcome instead.

2. Delivery and retry semantics

Guarantee: The service retries on timeouts, network errors, and these status codes: 408, 425, 429, and any 5xx.

Guarantee: The service does not retry other 4xx responses. Use 400 for malformed or unverifiable deliveries you do not want retried, and 429 or 503 when you want a retry after a transient overload.

Guarantee: A failing delivery is retried up to five times after the initial attempt (six attempts total). The nominal schedule is approximately 1 minute, 5 minutes, 30 minutes, 2 hours, and 12 hours after the prior attempt. Intervals are approximate and are not a timing guarantee.

Recommendation: Return 2xx quickly and work asynchronously. If you cannot finish within 10 seconds, enqueue the event and return 2xx immediately.

Recommendation: Do not rely on redirects. Whether the sender follows redirects is currently unconfirmed (see Section 6); configure your webhook URL as the final HTTPS endpoint.

3. Ordering and idempotency

Guarantee: Duplicates are possible. The same event ID may be delivered more than once because of retries and manual replays.

Guarantee: Delivery order is not guaranteed. Events usually arrive in creation order, but concurrent retries can reorder them.

Recommendation: Treat every handler as idempotent: key processed work by X-Orbit-Event, deduplicate before side effects, and make repeated deliveries safe. If ordering matters, include your own sequence or timestamp check per entity and tolerate late or reordered arrivals rather than assuming arrival order.

Guarantee: Operators can replay an event from the dashboard for up to 7 days after its original delivery. A replay carries the same event ID and must be handled as a duplicate of the original.

4. Secret rotation

Guarantee: The webhook secret is shown only once when created. Store it securely on receipt.

Guarantee: During rotation, two secrets are valid simultaneously for 24 hours. The UI labels them current and previous.

Recommendation: On each delivery, try the current secret first and fall back to the previous secret during the rotation window. Complete the cutover within 24 hours, then remove the old secret from your configuration. Rotate promptly if a secret may have been exposed.

5. Operating a receiver

Recommendation: Expose a dedicated HTTPS route, validate that the payload is under 512 KiB before buffering unbounded input, and optionally check for User-Agent: Orbit-Hooks/2 as a routing hint — never as authentication. Authentication is the HMAC signature only.

Recommendation: Monitor verification failures, 429/5xx rates, latency, duplicates, and per-entity sequence gaps. A sustained rise in retries or signature failures usually means a secret mismatch, clock problem, or overload.

Recommendation: Deploy safely: drain in-flight requests, keep both secrets configured across a secret change, and test rotation and replay in staging first.

6. Needs product confirmation

The following is explicitly unresolved; do not build on an assumption:

  • Redirect behavior: Security expects redirects are not followed, but the implementation owner has not confirmed it. Until confirmed, assume redirects may fail and use a final URL.

7. Troubleshooting

Symptom Likely cause What to do
Signature mismatch on every event Signing parsed JSON instead of timestamp + "." + raw bytes; wrong secret; whitespace or encoding change Sign raw bytes per Section 1; re-check current/previous secret; log event ID and headers, not the secret
Valid events rejected after rotation Receiver still uses only the old secret Accept both current and previous secrets for 24 hours, then remove the old one
Retries continue after your handler ran Handler returned non-2xx, timed out after 10 seconds, or returned 429/5xx Return 2xx within 10 seconds; move slow work to a queue; use 4xx other than 408/425/429 to stop retries
Same event processed twice At-least-once delivery; retry or 7-day dashboard replay Deduplicate by X-Orbit-Event before side effects
Events arrive out of order Concurrent retries; order is not guaranteed Do not assume arrival order; sequence per entity in your own store
Large payload rejected or truncated Body exceeds the 512 KiB maximum Enforce the limit, return 2xx only after accepting the full body, and fetch large objects out of band if needed
Deliveries stop after a URL move Endpoint redirect behavior is unconfirmed Update the webhook URL to the final HTTPS URL instead of relying on a redirect

hy4-preview/05-technical-writing/RESPONSE.md

Editorial notes — WEBHOOKS.md

Editorial choices

Structure. Ordered by what an integrator does first: verify a signature → delivery/retry semantics → ordering/idempotency → key rotation → operations → troubleshooting → open questions. The signature section leads because a wrong verification implementation is the most common and most costly integration failure.

Guarantees vs. recommendations. Used explicit bold labels (Guaranteed, Recommended, Not guaranteed, Unconfirmed) on every claim rather than hedging with adverbs. Anything not sourced from the notes is marked recommended — e.g. the 300-second timestamp skew window, acknowledge-then-queue pattern, and the alerting advice.

Verification example. Node.js (node:crypto), because it can show a real constant-time comparison in a few lines without third-party dependencies. It covers the four things that go wrong in practice:

  1. raw bytes (not reparsed JSON),
  2. timestamp + "." + body as the signing string,
  3. constant-time comparison,
  4. multiple valid secrets, so the same function works during rotation.

I deliberately did not include a second language; two examples would have pushed the document past the word budget without adding information.

Conflict: what is signed. The notes say old docs claim parsed-JSON signing and that the current implementation signs timestamp + "." + raw body. I treated the current implementation as authoritative, documented only that scheme, and added a visible callout saying the legacy note is stale. I did not tell readers to accept both schemes by default — that weakens security — but I allowed dual acceptance only for anyone still supporting the old system, with a "retire this path" instruction.

Conflict/ambiguity: retry count. "Schedule roughly 1m, 5m, 30m, 2h, 12h" and "five retries after initial try" agree (five intervals = five retries), so I stated it as six attempts total. Because the notes say "roughly", I explicitly told readers not to build logic on exact spacing.

Conservative reading of ordering. The notes say order "usually follows creation" but is not guaranteed. I classified ordering as Not guaranteed rather than "usually ordered" — typical behavior is not a contract, and encouraging developers to rely on it causes production bugs.

4xx semantics. I surfaced the operational consequence of non-retried 4xx: silent, unrecoverable data loss. This is the least obvious part of the retry policy and the one most likely to bite a customer.

Security guidance. Added the warning against logging secrets, signatures, or full payloads, and tied it to the rotation section (payloads may contain customer data).

Redirects. Treated as unresolved. The notes record disagreement between Security and an unconfirmed implementation owner, so I did not state a behavior. Instead: a "do not rely on this" entry in the open-questions list, a "register a final URL" recommendation, and a troubleshooting row. Inventing either answer would have been worse than flagging it.

Troubleshooting table. Written symptom → cause → action, and seeded from the failure modes implied by the semantics (raw-body mutation, rotation mismatch, duplicates, out-of-order, abandoned 4xx, timeout-under-load, 512 KiB limit) so it diagnoses real problems rather than restating the spec.

Unresolved questions for product

  1. Are 3xx redirects followed? Security says no; implementation owner unconfirmed. Blocks a definitive statement.
  2. How is a 3xx response from the receiver treated — success, permanent failure, or retryable? Not covered by the retry list at all.
  3. Is Retry-After honored on 429? Affects whether customers should send it; currently unmentioned.
  4. Exact retry timing / jitter. "Roughly" is not specifiable for customers building alerting thresholds.
  5. Is any legacy traffic still using parsed-JSON signing? Before we deprecate the note entirely.
  6. Is the 10-second timeout measured to first byte or to full response? Assumed full response; matters for streaming endpoints.
  7. What happens after the fifth retry — dead-letter, alert, or silent drop? Not stated; customers need to know whether they can detect exhaustion.
  8. Is the 24-hour two-secret overlap automatic on rotation, or must it be triggered in the UI? The notes say "two secrets valid for 24 hours" without saying who starts the clock.

Write RESPONSE.md with your editorial choices and unresolved questions. Work only in this directory.

hy4-preview/05-technical-writing/WEBHOOKS.md

Receiving Orbit Webhooks

This guide explains how to accept, verify, and operate against webhook deliveries from the Orbit service: HTTP POST requests with a JSON body, sent to the endpoint you configure.

  • Guaranteed behavior is part of the delivery contract; build against it.
  • Recommended behavior is advice, not a guarantee.
  • Unconfirmed items are listed at the end; do not rely on them.

1. Verifying a signature (start here)

Every delivery carries:

Header Meaning
X-Orbit-Event Unique event id (stable across replays)
X-Orbit-Type Event type, e.g. invoice.paid
X-Orbit-Timestamp Unix seconds when the request was signed
X-Orbit-Signature v1=<hex> HMAC-SHA256 signature

The signature is HMAC-SHA256 over the raw request bytes, not over reparsed JSON. The signing string is <X-Orbit-Timestamp> + "." + <raw body>.

Your body parser must therefore preserve the raw payload before any JSON decoding. Most frameworks (express.json(), ASP.NET model binding) replace the raw stream; capture it first (e.g. express.raw({ type: "application/json" })) or verification will fail intermittently.

import crypto from "node:crypto";

const MAX_SKEW_SECONDS = 300; // recommended, not a service-enforced limit

// secrets: array of currently valid secrets (see "Key rotation")
export function verifyOrbitSignature(rawBody, headers, secrets) {
  const timestamp = headers["x-orbit-timestamp"];
  const signature = headers["x-orbit-signature"];
  if (!timestamp || !signature) return false;

  const age = Math.abs(Math.floor(Date.now() / 1000) - Number(timestamp));
  if (!Number.isFinite(age) || age > MAX_SKEW_SECONDS) return false; // replay guard

  return secrets.some((secret) => {
    const expected =
      "v1=" +
      crypto
        .createHmac("sha256", secret)
        .update(`${timestamp}.${rawBody}`)
        .digest("hex");
    const a = Buffer.from(expected, "utf8");
    const b = Buffer.from(signature, "utf8");
    return a.length === b.length && crypto.timingSafeEqual(a, b);
  });
}

Compare with a constant-time function (timingSafeEqual, hmac.compare_digest) — never ===. Return 401 on failure.

Documentation conflict (resolved). Legacy docs state the signature covers the parsed JSON body. That is stale: the current implementation signs timestamp + "." + raw body, as above. Accept the legacy scheme only if you must support the old system, and retire that path.

2. Delivery and retries

Guaranteed. Your endpoint has 10 seconds to return any 2xx; the response body is ignored. Anything else — non-2xx, timeout, or network error — is a failed attempt.

Orbit retries automatically after connection/timeout/network errors, and after 408, 425, 429, or any 5xx. Other 4xx responses are not retried and the delivery is abandoned; treat them as permanent failures.

The schedule is approximately 1 min, 5 min, 30 min, 2 h, 12 h after the initial attempt — five retries, six attempts total. Intervals are approximate; do not build logic that depends on exact spacing.

Requests use User-Agent: Orbit-Hooks/2 and a maximum payload of 512 KiB. Do not rely on the webhook body for large content; fetch the resource from the API instead.

Recommended. Acknowledge first, process later: verify, persist the event id, enqueue, and return 200 within a few hundred milliseconds. Do the real work in a background job. This keeps you inside the 10-second budget and avoids cascading retries.

3. Ordering and idempotency

Not guaranteed. Deliveries usually follow event creation order, but concurrent retries mean order is not guaranteed, and duplicates are possible — a timeout after you processed the event produces a retry.

Recommended. Make handlers idempotent on X-Orbit-Event: store it with a unique constraint, and on conflict return 200 without reprocessing. Never assume event N precedes N+1; if ordering matters, re-read current state from the API. This also covers the replay UI, which guarantees resends with the same event id for 7 days.

4. Key rotation

The signing secret is shown once; store it in a secret manager.

Guaranteed. During rotation two secrets are valid for 24 hours; the UI labels them current and previous.

Recommended procedure: add the new secret to your verifier alongside the old one (accepting either — see the array-based example above), deploy, promote the new secret in the UI, then remove the previous secret after 24 hours.

Never log secrets, signatures, or full payloads — log the event id and type only; payloads may contain customer data.

5. Operational advice

  • Return 2xx fast; alert on sustained non-2xx rates.
  • Alert on abandoned (non-retried 4xx) deliveries — silent data loss.
  • Track lag via X-Orbit-Timestamp; a growing gap means you are behind.
  • Keep an idempotency/dead-letter store for safe reprocessing.
  • Use the replay UI (7 days) to recover from your own outages.

6. Troubleshooting

Symptom Likely cause Action
Signature mismatch on every request Wrong secret, or signing over parsed JSON Sign timestamp + "." + raw body; confirm you copied the secret correctly
Signature mismatch only on some requests Body parser mutated/normalized JSON before verification Capture raw bytes (express.raw, verify hook) before JSON parsing
Signature mismatch after rotation Old secret removed too early Accept both secrets for the full 24-hour overlap
Repeated deliveries of the same event Timeout after successful processing (expected duplicates) Make handler idempotent on X-Orbit-Event; return 200 on conflict
Events processed out of order Concurrent retries (order not guaranteed) Don't depend on order; re-read state from the API
Event delivered once, never retried You returned a non-retried 4xx, or a parsing error Return 5xx for transient failures; reserve 4xx for permanent ones
Timeouts under load Synchronous work inside the request handler Acknowledge, enqueue, process asynchronously
Replay option unavailable for old event Outside the 7-day replay window Recover via the API
413 / truncated payload Payload approaches or exceeds the 512 KiB limit Send references, not full content; fetch details from the API
Redirect expected to work Redirect handling is unconfirmed (see below) Use a direct https URL that responds 2xx without redirecting

7. Open questions (do not rely on these)

  1. Redirects. Whether Orbit follows 3xx redirects is unconfirmed: Security's position is no, the implementation owner has not confirmed. Assume redirects are not followed; register a final URL.
  2. How a 3xx from your endpoint is treated (success, failure, or retryable) is unspecified.
  3. Exact retry timing — the schedule is "roughly" as listed; jitter is not specified.
  4. Retry-After on 429 — whether Orbit honors it is unconfirmed.
  5. Legacy parsed-JSON signing — assumed stale; confirm no production traffic still uses it before deprecating.

Confirm these with product before publishing externally.

  • Correct Python example
  • Troubleshooting covers legacy-doc case

Weaknesses

  • 'Treat the timestamp as an opaque identifier... not a clock' and 'dead-letter your own undeliverable events' are confusing

deepseek-v4-pro/05-technical-writing/RESPONSE.md

Editorial choices and unresolved questions

Editorial choices

  • Signature algorithm conflict. The notes contain two accounts: an older document saying the signature is over parsed JSON, and the current implementation signing timestamp + "." + raw body. I treated the current implementation as authoritative, documented the raw-body algorithm, and flagged the parsed-JSON description as outdated rather than presenting both as equally valid.
  • Timestamp as signing input. I described the X-Orbit-Timestamp value as the string that is prepended to the raw body with a dot separator, and recommended treating it as an opaque signature component rather than a clock.
  • Retry schedule. The notes say "five retries after initial try" followed by the schedule 1m, 5m, 30m, 2h, 12h (five entries). I presented these as the five retries, labeled "approximately," since the source uses "roughly."
  • Guarantees vs. recommendations. I labeled statements explicitly as guarantee, recommendation, or unconfirmed so readers can distinguish enforced behavior from advice.
  • Language for the example. I used Python because hmac.compare_digest makes the constant-time comparison requirement idiomatic and short. The example reads the raw body as bytes and never parses before verifying.
  • Replay. I included the 7-day same-ID replay as a source of duplicate deliveries under ordering/idempotency rather than omitting it, since it directly strengthens the idempotency recommendation.
  • Redirect behavior. Marked as unconfirmed in the guide rather than inventing a policy.

Unresolved questions requiring product confirmation

  1. Redirects. Whether HTTP redirects are followed (and to what depth/limit) is explicitly uncertain in the source; the security and implementation teams disagree. Final behavior needs confirmation.
  2. Timestamp canonicalization. Whether the signing input uses the timestamp exactly as emitted in the header (decimal seconds, no padding) or a stricter canonical form. I assumed the header string is used verbatim.
  3. Secret overlap window. Whether the "two secrets valid for 24 hours" window is a fixed 24-hour rotation, or an overlap that is configurable / per-secret from creation time.
  4. Retry schedule precision. Whether the 1m, 5m, 30m, 2h, 12h intervals are fixed or jittered, and whether retries are strictly capped at five.
  5. Replay scope. Whether the 7-day replay always reuses the original event ID and signature, or can regenerate/update timestamps or signature inputs.

deepseek-v4-pro/05-technical-writing/WEBHOOKS.md

Orbit Webhooks

This guide describes how to integrate with Orbit webhooks. It covers signature verification, delivery and retry behavior, ordering and idempotency, secret rotation, and operational advice. Each section labels statements as guarantees (behavior you can rely on) or recommendations (best practices we suggest but do not enforce).

Overview

Orbit delivers each event as an HTTP POST with a JSON body to your configured endpoint. The following headers accompany every delivery:

  • X-Orbit-Event — the event ID.
  • X-Orbit-Type — the event type.
  • X-Orbit-Timestamp — the send time in Unix seconds.
  • X-Orbit-Signature — the signature, in the form v1=<hex>.

Requests identify themselves with User-Agent: Orbit-Hooks/2. The JSON payload is at most 512 KiB.

Verifying signatures

Orbit signs each request with HMAC-SHA256 over the message timestamp + "." + raw body, where timestamp is the string value of X-Orbit-Timestamp (Unix seconds) and raw body is the exact request body, byte for byte.

Guarantee: the signature is computed over the raw body bytes. Verify the signature before parsing or re-serializing the JSON, since reformatting changes the bytes and breaks the check. Use a constant-time comparison to avoid timing leaks.

A minimal verification example in Python:

import hashlib
import hmac

def verify(raw_body: bytes, signature: str, timestamp: str, secret: str) -> bool:
    message = timestamp.encode() + b"." + raw_body
    expected = "v1=" + hmac.new(secret.encode(), message, hashlib.sha256).hexdigest()
    return hmac.compare_digest(expected, signature)

Connect this to your framework by reading the raw request body as bytes and passing the timestamp header unchanged. In the example, secret must match the signing secret shown by our dashboard.

Note: older documentation described signing the parsed JSON instead of the raw body. That description is outdated, and the raw-body algorithm above matches the current implementation. If your integration followed the older guidance, re-verify against the raw body before going live.

Delivery and retry semantics

Guarantee: Orbit retries a delivery on timeouts, network errors, and HTTP status codes 408, 425, 429, and any 5xx. It does not retry any other 4xx response. After the initial attempt, five retries are scheduled at approximately 1 minute, 5 minutes, 30 minutes, 2 hours, and 12 hours.

Guarantee: your endpoint has 10 seconds to return any 2xx status. The response body is ignored, so return a minimal 200 OK once the event is committed.

Unconfirmed: whether deliveries follow HTTP redirects. Do not rely on redirect responses for delivery; if your endpoint must move, update the configured URL instead.

Ordering and idempotency

Guarantee: deliveries are not guaranteed to be unique or strictly ordered. The same event may be delivered more than once, and although events are usually sent in creation order, concurrent retries can reorder them. In addition, the dashboard replay feature can resend an event (using the same event ID) for up to 7 days, which is another source of duplicate deliveries.

Recommendation: treat each event as at-least-once and make your consumer idempotent. Persist the X-Orbit-Event ID and skip processing if you have already handled it. Because ordering is not guaranteed, do not rely on arrival order for correctness.

Secret rotation

Guarantee: the dashboard supports two simultaneously valid signing secrets for 24 hours, labeled current and previous. The new secret is shown only once; store it securely at creation time.

Recommendation: rotate by adding a new secret, verifying that production traffic signed with the new secret verifies correctly, then retiring the old one. Accepting both secrets during the overlap prevents dropped events from in-flight requests.

Operational advice

  • Do not log the signing secret or full sensitive payloads; log only fields you need for debugging.
  • Respond with 2xx only after the event is durably stored so retries are idempotent rather than lossy.
  • Treat the timestamp as an opaque identifier for signature input, not a clock for your business logic.
  • Monitor webhook failure rates and dead-letter your own undeliverable events.

Troubleshooting

Symptom Likely cause Remedy
Signature mismatch Secret wrong or body reformatted before verification Verify with raw body bytes; confirm the secret matches the dashboard
Signature mismatch after deploying old integration Parsing JSON before signing Use the raw-body algorithm; ignore outdated parsed-JSON docs
No retries after a 4xx Other 4xx codes are not retried Return 5xx/429 for transient failures, 2xx on success
Events arriving out of order Concurrent retries reorder deliveries Do not rely on order; key idempotency on event ID
Duplicate event processed twice At-least-once delivery Deduplicate on X-Orbit-Event
Delivery times out Endpoint slower than 10 seconds Return 2xx immediately and process asynchronously

RESPONSE — editorial choices and open questions

What I wrote and why

WEBHOOKS.md is a developer-facing integration guide built strictly from SOURCE_NOTES.md. It covers: delivery format and headers, a minimal working signature-verification example (Python/Flask, hashing the raw body and using hmac.compare_digest), response and retry semantics, ordering/duplicates and idempotency guidance, secret rotation, replay behavior, operational recommendations, and a troubleshooting table. Length is within the 700–1,100 word target (roughly 1,000 words of prose and code).

Editorial choices

  • Guarantees vs. recommendations. I labeled statements explicitly. Guarantees come straight from the notes (what is retried, retry count and approximate schedule, 10-second window, 512 KiB cap, two-secret/24-hour rotation, 7-day replay with the same event ID, raw-body signing). Recommendations are advice layered on top: timestamp-skew checks, idempotency-key deduplication, async processing, not logging secrets or sensitive payloads, monitoring, and avoiding redirects.
  • Contradiction resolution — signature scheme. The notes say old docs describe signing parsed JSON while the current implementation signs timestamp + "." + raw body. I resolved this conservatively: the guide instructs raw-body signing (matching the current implementation and the timestamp header's existence) and explicitly flags the older guidance as stale rather than silently dropping it, so readers who saw the old docs know which to trust.
  • Retry math. "Five retries after initial try" is stated as six total attempts; the troubleshooting table's "~13.5 hours" is the sum of the approximate intervals (1m+5m+30m+2h+12h), framed as approximate since the schedule itself is approximate.
  • Idempotent example. The minimal example verifies, checks timestamp skew, and returns 204 without processing, with a comment pointing to the idempotency section — kept minimal per the task while remaining safe to copy.
  • Redirect uncertainty. Rather than inventing behavior, the guide states that redirect handling is unconfirmed, advises assuming redirects are not followed (the conservative, security-aligned reading), and lists it as an open item.

Unresolved questions for product/engineering

  1. Redirects: Does the delivery client follow 3xx responses (and if so, how many hops, and is the signature re-sent to the redirect target)? Security believes no; the implementation owner has not confirmed.
  2. Timestamp enforcement: Is the timestamp validated server-side at all (i.e., does Orbit sign a fresh timestamp per retry, and is there any canonical tolerance)? The guide's 5-minute skew is a recommendation only.
  3. Retry jitter/exactness: Are the 1m/5m/30m/2h/12h intervals fixed or jittered? The guide says "approximate," but confirmed behavior would let us tighten the wording.
  4. Payload size at 512 KiB: What happens to events exceeding the cap — dropped, truncated, or delivered differently? Not covered by the notes.
  5. Replay signature: Do replays reuse the original timestamp/signature inputs, or are they re-signed with a fresh timestamp? This affects whether strict timestamp-skew checks could reject legitimate replays — worth confirming before customers adopt tight skew windows.

kimi-k3/05-technical-writing/WEBHOOKS.md

Receiving Orbit Webhooks

Orbit delivers event notifications to your endpoint as HTTP POST requests with a JSON body. This guide covers how to verify deliveries, what delivery behavior is guaranteed, and how to operate a robust receiver. Throughout, guarantees describe behavior the service commits to; recommendations are operational advice you should follow but that the service does not enforce.

Delivery format

Each delivery is a POST with these headers:

Header Meaning
X-Orbit-Event Unique event ID. Stable across retries and replays.
X-Orbit-Type Event type (e.g., invoice.paid).
X-Orbit-Timestamp Unix seconds when the delivery was signed.
X-Orbit-Signature v1=<hex> — HMAC-SHA-256 signature (see below).

Requests are sent with User-Agent: Orbit-Hooks/2. Payloads are capped at 512 KiB.

Verifying signatures (guarantee)

Orbit signs every delivery with the endpoint secret shown to you once in the dashboard. The signed content is:

<X-Orbit-Timestamp> + "." + <raw request body bytes>

Compute HMAC-SHA-256 over that exact string with your secret, hex-encode it, and compare it against the <hex> value in X-Orbit-Signature (after the v1= prefix) using a constant-time comparison.

Two details matter:

  1. Sign the raw bytes, not parsed JSON. Re-serializing a parsed body changes whitespace and key order and will produce mismatches. (Older documentation described signing the parsed JSON; that guidance is stale — the current implementation signs timestamp + "." + raw body as above.)
  2. Check the timestamp (recommendation): reject deliveries whose X-Orbit-Timestamp is more than a few minutes old to limit replay windows. Since the timestamp is part of the signed content, an attacker cannot alter it without invalidating the signature.

Minimal verification example (Python/Flask)

import hashlib, hmac, time
from flask import Flask, request, abort

app = Flask(__name__)
SECRET = b"whsec_..."          # store in a secret manager, never in code
MAX_SKEW = 300                  # 5 minutes

@app.post("/webhooks/orbit")
def orbit_hook():
    ts = request.headers.get("X-Orbit-Timestamp", "")
    sig = request.headers.get("X-Orbit-Signature", "")
    body = request.get_data()   # raw bytes — do not parse first

    expected = "v1=" + hmac.new(
        SECRET, f"{ts}.".encode() + body, hashlib.sha256
    ).hexdigest()

    if not hmac.compare_digest(expected, sig):
        abort(401)
    if abs(time.time() - int(ts)) > MAX_SKEW:
        abort(401)

    event_id = request.headers["X-Orbit-Event"]
    # ... enqueue / process idempotently (see below) ...
    return "", 204

Recommendation: never log your signing secret or full payloads containing sensitive data. Log the event ID and type instead.

Responding and retries

Your endpoint has 10 seconds to respond with any 2xx status. The response body is ignored, so return quickly (a bare 204 is fine) and process the event asynchronously.

What is retried (guarantee)

Orbit retries a delivery when your endpoint:

  • times out or fails at the network level (connection refused, reset, DNS), or
  • returns 408, 425, 429, or any 5xx status.

Other 4xx statuses are not retried — they are treated as permanent rejections. If your endpoint cannot accept a class of events, return a non-retryable 4xx deliberately rather than letting the delivery retry to exhaustion.

There are five retries after the initial attempt (six attempts total), on an approximate schedule of 1 minute, 5 minutes, 30 minutes, 2 hours, and 12 hours after the previous attempt. Treat these intervals as approximate; do not build logic that depends on exact timing.

Ordering, duplicates, and idempotency

  • Duplicates are possible. At-least-once delivery plus retries means the same event can arrive more than once.
  • Order is not guaranteed. Delivery usually follows event creation order, but concurrent retries can reorder events.

Recommendation: make your handler idempotent. Before processing, check whether you have already processed the X-Orbit-Event ID; store processed IDs (e.g., in a database table or cache) and skip duplicates. Where ordering matters, use a monotonic field inside the payload (such as an updated-at timestamp or version) to discard stale events rather than assuming arrival order is authoritative.

Secret rotation

Orbit supports zero-downtime rotation: two secrets are valid at the same time for 24 hours, labeled current and previous in the dashboard. To rotate:

  1. Generate a new secret in the dashboard. The old secret remains valid as previous for 24 hours.
  2. Update your receiver to accept signatures from both secrets during the overlap (try the new secret first, fall back to the old).
  3. After the 24-hour window, remove the old secret from your configuration.

Replays

The dashboard replay UI can resend any event for up to 7 days. Replays use the same event ID as the original delivery, so the idempotency check above also protects you from accidental reprocessing of replays — or, if you want replays to reprocess, you can distinguish them operationally by forcing a reprocess of a known ID rather than by trusting that replays look different.

Operational recommendations

  • Return 2xx within 10 seconds; offload work to a queue.
  • Monitor your 4xx/5xx rates; sustained 5xx burns through the retry schedule and events are dropped after the final attempt.
  • Alert on signature-verification failures — they indicate a wrong secret or possible tampering.
  • Serve a stable, publicly reachable HTTPS URL. Whether Orbit follows HTTP redirects (3xx) is unconfirmed; do not rely on redirects. If your URL changes, update the endpoint configuration in the dashboard instead.

Troubleshooting

Symptom Likely cause Fix
Signature always mismatches Body parsed/re-serialized before hashing, or wrong secret Hash timestamp + "." + raw body; confirm current/previous secret
Signature fails only during rotation Receiver only knows one secret Accept both secrets during the 24-hour overlap
Deliveries stop after ~13.5 hours All 6 attempts exhausted (5xx/timeout) Fix endpoint health; use replay UI (events kept 7 days)
Same event processed twice Retried delivery or replay not deduplicated Deduplicate on X-Orbit-Event
Events arrive out of order Concurrent retries Use payload timestamps/versions, not arrival order
Non-retryable error keeps resending Your endpoint returns 408/429/5xx or times out Return a different 4xx for permanent rejections
Requests rejected as too large Payload over 512 KiB Not fixable receiver-side; contact Orbit about payload size

Open item

  • Redirect handling: whether Orbit follows 3xx redirects is not confirmed by the implementation owner. Until it is, assume redirects are not followed and keep endpoint URLs stable.

Labelled guide that promotes several inferences to Guarantees (retry id reuse, permanent stop after exhaustion, replay window anchored to first delivery, 'only current accepted') and ships a 300 s freshness check that conflicts with its own 12-hour retry schedule.

Strengths

  • Guarantee/Recommendation/Unconfirmed legend
  • Troubleshooting covers exhaustion and non-retried 4xx

Weaknesses

  • 'Guarantee: The same event always carries the same X-Orbit-Event id - across... retries' not in source
  • Example rejects anything older than 300 s, which would reject later retries if timestamps are not refreshed; not flagged
  • 'about 12 hours' / 'stop after ~12 hours' understates a ~14.6 h span
  • 'Very large events fail... Keep events under the limit' invents a failure mode the customer cannot control; no open-questions section

  • h2.py: Q fresh=True, 1h-old retry=False
